#### 9.68 — bicyclo[3.1.0]hexane skeleton (as in sabinene, nutmeg/pepper aroma), fn 1, Aust. J. Chem. 1987, 40, 1321–1325
- SM 1: 1-methoxy-4-methylcyclohexa-1,4-diene `COC1=CCC(C)=CC1` (C8H12O). Excess HCl → 2 (C8H14Cl2O); "two steps" → 3: 5-methylbicyclo[3.1.0]hexan-2-one (drawn as bicyclic ketone with bridgehead methyl) `CC12CC1C(=O)CC2` [M on exact drawing].
- Asks: structure of 2 and mechanism of its formation.
- Answer [H]: 2 = 1,4-dichloro-1-methoxy-4-methylcyclohexane `COC1(Cl)CCC(C)(Cl)CC1` (formula verified). Mechanism: two Markovnikov HCl additions — protonation of enol-ether C=C gives O-stabilized oxocarbenium → Cl⁻ (α-chloro ether); protonation of trisubstituted C=C gives 3° cation → Cl⁻. (Then: hydrolysis of α-chloro ether to 4-chloro-4-methylcyclohexanone; base → enolate intramolecular SN2 at C4 → cyclopropane = 3.)

#### 9.82 — Taxol (from *Taxus brevifolia*), K. C. Nicolaou total synthesis, fn 2, Nature 1994, 367, 630–634 (도전 문제)
- SM 1: Nicolaou tricyclic taxane intermediate: A-ring tetrasubstituted alkene, C9 ketone, C10-OAc, C1–C2 cyclic carbonate, C7-OBn (wedge), C-ring disubstituted alkene C5=C6, C4 quaternary bearing acetonide (with C20 CH2O). SMILES uncertain. Conditions: 1) BH3·THF 2) H2O2, basic → alcohol 2 (regio- and stereoselective) → several steps → Taxol 3.
- Asks: (a) which functional group reacts with BH3 and why; (b) predict regio/stereochem considering bulky benzyl group; draw 2.
- Answer [M]: (a) only the disubstituted C-ring C5=C6 alkene; the A-ring alkene is tetrasubstituted and very hindered (bridgehead-like), ketone/esters/carbonate react much slower with BH3. (b) B adds to C5 (remote from the bulky C7-OBn, adjacent to C4), H to C6 → C5-OH (needed for taxol's oxetane at C5); syn addition from the face opposite to the benzyl ether (α face, since OBn is β-wedge) → C5α-OH (hashed), H's cis. Stereo face assignment medium/low confidence.

#### 9.83 — adamantylideneadamantane bromonium ion, fn 3, J. Am. Chem. Soc. 1985, 107, 4504–4508
- SM: adamantylideneadamantane `C1C2CC3CC1CC(C2)C3=C1C2CC3CC(C2)CC1C3`; Br2 → stable bromonium ion (high yield; with Br⁻/Br3⁻ counterion); no further attack by Br⁻ to give dibromide.
- Asks: draw energy diagram consistent with this and compare with normal bromination (e.g. propylene).
- Answer [H]: Backside attack on either carbon is blocked by the adamantyl cages → second step has prohibitively high barrier; bromonium ion sits in a deep well (stable, isolable). For propylene: bromonium is a shallow high-energy intermediate rapidly opened by Br⁻ → overall exergonic to the vicinal dibromide.

#### 9.84 — diisopinocampheylborane (Ipc2BH), fn 4, J. Org. Chem. 1984, 49, 945–947
- SM: (+)-α-pinene (drawn single enantiomer) `CC1=CCC2CC1C2(C)C`; BH3·THF 0.5 equiv → Ipc2BH (one stereoisomer).
- Asks: why only one stereoisomer of Ipc2BH forms.
- Answer [H]: α-pinene is enantiopure; syn hydroboration occurs exclusively from the face anti to the gem-dimethyl bridge (other face blocked), B to less substituted C (anti-Markovnikov) → each pinene gives the same configuration at the three new/existing centres; both Ipc groups identical → single stereoisomer (chiral, used in asymmetric hydroboration).

#### 9.85 — zaragozic acid A (squalene synthase inhibitor; isolated 1992 from fungal cultures), fn 5, Angew. Chem. Int. Ed. 1994, 33, 2187–2190 (Nicolaou)
- SM 1: spiro bicyclic: γ-butyrolactone with exocyclic alkylidene =CH–CH2OH at C3, spiro-fused (C2) to 2,2-dimethyl-1,3-dioxolane bearing CH2OSEM (SEM = CH2OCH2CH2SiMe3). Cat. OsO4, NMO → triol 2 (syn dihydroxylation from top face only) → rapid rearrangement (translactonization) → 3 (lactone now formed to the new secondary OH; free CH2OH arms).
- Asks: justify the diastereofacial selectivity (top face, not bottom).
- Answer [M]: bottom face of the alkene is shielded by the spiro acetonide (the dioxolane O/gem-dimethyl and the CH2OSEM substituent are oriented under the π-bond in the rigid spirocycle), so OsO4 approaches from the less hindered top face; syn addition delivers both OH from that face. Build model.

#### 9.86 — monensin (from *Streptomyces cinnamonensis*), W. C. Still synthesis, fn 6, J. Am. Chem. Soc. 1980, 102, 2118–2120
- SM 1: (Z)-alkenoic acid: HO2C(1)–CH(2)(CH3)–CH2(3)–CH(4)(CH3)–CH(5)=CH(6)–CH2–CH(CH3)–(4-yl-2,2-dimethyl-1,3-dioxolane); Z C5=C6. I2, NaHCO3 → 2: δ-lactone (6-membered: O1–C5), 2,4-dimethyl, with C6 bearing I (exocyclic CHI–CH2–...), single diastereomer (5S,6S); (5R,6R) not observed.
- Asks: (a) mechanism of iodolactonization; (b) rationalize via C4–C5 Newman projection (lowest-energy conformer).
- Answer [M-H]: (a) I2 forms iodonium on C5=C6; carboxylate (deprotonated by NaHCO3) attacks C5 anti (6-exo, Markovnikov-like at the carbon giving 6-ring) → trans addition of O and I. (b) allylic 1,3-strain (A1,3) of the Z-alkene forces C4–H to eclipse the C=C (C4-H in plane, syn to C6 substituent); the C4-methyl and C3 chain sit on opposite faces; I⁺ attacks the face anti to the bulkier substituent/from the face allowing the C3–CO2⁻ chain to reach C5 → only (5S,6S).

#### 9.87 — relative rates of oxymercuration with Hg(OAc)2, fn 7, J. Am. Chem. Soc. 1989, 111, 1414–1418
- Data: CH2=C(CH3)CH2CH2CH3 1000; CH2=CHCH2CH2CH2CH3 100; CH2=CHCH2OMe 31.8; CH(CH3)=C(CH3)2 25.8; CH2=CHCH2Cl 2.4.
- Asks: explain trend structurally.
- Answer [M-H]: electrophilic addition → more electron-rich alkene/more stabilized cationic (mercurinium) TS faster: 1,1-disubstituted (2 alkyls, 3°-like) > monosubstituted. Allylic electronegative OMe, Cl withdraw inductively → slower (Cl > OMe withdrawing). Trisubstituted 4 is slower than expected because of steric hindrance to the bulky Hg(OAc)2 (and mercurinium formation at a more crowded C).

#### 9.88 — relative rates of hydroboration with 9-BBN, fn 8, J. Am. Chem. Soc. 1989, 111, 1414–1418 (same citation printed as fn 7)
- Data: CH2=CHOBu 1615; CH2=CHBu 100; CH2=CHCH2OMe 32.5; CH2=CHOAc 22.8; CH2=CHCH2OAc 21.9; CH2=CHCH2CN 5.9; CH2=CHCH2Cl 4.0; cis-2-butene 0.95; trans-CH3CH2CH=CHCl 0.003.
- Asks: explain (a) 1–5, (b) 5–7, (c) 2, 8, 9.
- Answer [M]: hydroboration is electrophilic (B electrophile, partial + on carbon in TS). (a) vinyl ether 1: O lone-pair resonance donation → most electron-rich → fastest; 2 alkyl reference; allylic OMe (3), vinyl OAc (4: acyl reduces O donation, induction withdraws), allylic OAc (5) inductively withdrawing → slower. (b) allylic EWG strength: CN and Cl withdraw more than OAc → 5 > 6 > 7. (c) bulky 9-BBN is very sensitive to sterics: monosubstituted 2 ≫ 1,2-disubstituted cis-2-butene 8; 9 is disubstituted AND has an electron-withdrawing vinylic Cl → slowest.

#### 9.89 — heliol (natural product), fn 9, Org. Lett. 2012, 14, 2929–2931
- SM: 2-(2-hydroxy-6-methylhept-6-en-2-yl)phenol `CC(O)(CCCC(C)=C)c1ccccc1O` (C14H20O2). H3O⁺, CH3CN → bridged benzoxabicycle (heliol skeleton) + enantiomer (racemic): `CC12CCCC(C)(C1)Oc1ccccc12` (C14H18O) [M].
- Asks: plausible mechanism.
- Answer [M-H]: protonate tertiary benzylic OH, lose H2O → tertiary benzylic cation; intramolecular attack by the 1,1-disubstituted alkene (terminal CH2 bonds to cation; Markovnikov) → six-membered carbocycle with new tertiary cation; phenol OH traps that cation (intramolecular) → oxonium; deprotonation → bridged ether. Racemic because cation planar/achiral SM.

#### 9.90 — reserpine (from *Rauwolfia serpentina*, Indian snakeroot), R. B. Woodward 1958 synthesis, fn 10, Tetrahedron 1958, 2, 1–57
- SM 1: tricyclic cis-fused γ-lactone (hydronaphthalene) with an allylic secondary OH (wedge) in the upper ring (adjacent alkene) and an isolated cyclohexene C=C in the lower ring; 1) Br2 2) NaOMe → short-lived 2 (ether-bridged, α,β-unsaturated lactone) → Michael addition of MeO⁻ → 3 (OMe added, ether bridge retained).
- Asks: mechanism 1 → 2.
- Answer [M-H]: Br2 adds to the lower-ring alkene forming a bromonium ion; the nearby OH attacks intramolecularly (anti opening) → cyclic ether bridge + C–Br (bromoetherification); NaOMe then removes the acidic H α to the lactone carbonyl and expels Br⁻ (E2/E1cB) → conjugated α,β-unsaturated lactone 2; MeO⁻ then adds conjugately (Michael) to give 3.

## Chapter 9 — 알켄의 첨가 반응 (Addition Reactions of Alkenes) — tail end (PDF p-25..p-29, book pp. 457–461)

The chapter starts before p-27, so its summary page is in the other agent's range (p-21 = book p. 453 shows the 개념 summary 9.6–9.14). Key reactions: HX addition (Markovnikov, carbocation and rearrangement); HBr/ROOR (anti-Markovnikov); acid-catalyzed hydration; oxymercuration–demercuration (Hg(OAc)2, H2O; NaBH4) → Markovnikov alcohol, no rearrangement (mercurinium ion); hydroboration–oxidation (BH3·THF; H2O2, NaOH) → anti-Markovnikov, syn; catalytic hydrogenation (H2, Pt/Pd/Ni; syn; asymmetric with chiral catalysts); halogenation X2 (bromonium ion, anti); halohydrin formation (X2, H2O); anti-dihydroxylation (RCO3H; H3O+); syn-dihydroxylation (OsO4/NMO or cold KMnO4); ozonolysis (O3; DMS).

### Problem 9.68 — book p. 458 (PDF p-26) — footnote 1 → Aust. J. Chem. 1987, 40, 1321–1325
- Topic: building a bicyclo[3.1.0]hexane ring, as found in natural flavor compounds such as sabinene (nutmeg).
- Starting material 1: 1-methoxy-4-methylcyclohexa-1,4-diene (Birch product of 4-methylanisole). SMILES `COC1=CCC(C)=CC1` (C8H12O) [H].
- Reagents: excess HCl → compound 2, C8H14Cl2O; then "two steps" → compound 3, a bicyclo[3.1.0]hexanone with a methyl at the bridgehead. The drawing shows a hexagon with a transannular bond from C(Me) to the carbon next to C=O, which reads as 5-methylbicyclo[3.1.0]hexan-2-one, `CC12CCC(=O)C1C2` [M].
- Asked: draw 2 and give a mechanism for its formation.
- Answer: Markovnikov addition of HCl to both C=C bonds. The enol ether is protonated to give an oxocarbenium ion, which Cl⁻ traps (α-chloro ether). The trisubstituted alkene is protonated to give a tertiary cation, which Cl⁻ traps. 2 = 1,4-dichloro-1-methoxy-4-methylcyclohexane, `COC1(Cl)CCC(C)(Cl)CC1`, C8H14Cl2O (formula checked) [H for constitution]. Likely two steps (not asked): hydrolysis to 4-chloro-4-methylcyclohexanone, then base-mediated enolate C-alkylation at C4 (1,3-closure) to make the cyclopropane [M].
- Concept: Markovnikov HX addition, carbocation/oxocarbenium stability.

### Problem 9.82 — book p. 459 (PDF p-27) — footnote 2 → Nature 1994, 367, 630–634 (K. C. Nicolaou Taxol total synthesis)
- Natural product: Taxol (paclitaxel, 3), from Taxus brevifolia.
- Starting material 1: an advanced taxane ABC tricyclic intermediate. A ring: tetrasubstituted bridgehead C11=C12 alkene with vinyl CH3, C10–OAc, C9 ketone, gem-dimethyl C15. C1/C2 cyclic carbonate. C ring: C7–OBn (benzyl ether), C5=C6 disubstituted ring alkene, and C4 quaternary as part of an acetonide (C4–O and C20 CH2–O, 2,2-dimethyl-1,3-dioxolane). C8 has an angular CH3, C3–H. SMILES uncertain (complex polycycle with stereocenters).
- Reagents: 1) BH3·THF; 2) H2O2, mild base → alcohol 2 (regio- and stereoselective). Then many steps → Taxol 3.
- Asked: (a) which functional groups in 1 can react with BH3, with reasons; (b) the benzyl group is very bulky and blocks hydroboration, so predict the regio- and stereochemistry and draw 2.
- Expected answer: (a) In principle BH3 can react with the two C=C bonds and the ketone. The tetrasubstituted bridgehead A-ring alkene is too hindered to react, and the C9 ketone is very hindered. The esters, carbonate, acetal and ether don't react. Only the disubstituted C5=C6 alkene reacts [H]. (b) Boron adds to C5, the carbon farther from the bulky C7–OBn (next to quaternary C4). The addition is syn, from the less hindered face, so 2 is the C5 secondary alcohol, with H added at C6. C5-oxygenation matches Taxol's oxetane oxygen at C5 [M; face assignment L–M].
- Concept: chemoselectivity of BH3; steric control of regiochemistry and of the face of syn addition in hydroboration.

### Problem 9.83 — book p. 459 (PDF p-27) — footnote 3 → J. Am. Chem. Soc. 1985, 107, 4504–4508
- Compound: adamantylideneadamantane, `C1C2CC3CC1CC(C2)C3=C1C2CC3CC(C2)CC1C3` (C20H28) [H].
- Reagent: Br2 → stable bromonium ion. Br⁻ cannot attack it from the back (SN2) to give the dibromide.
- Asked: draw an energy diagram consistent with this, and compare it with the diagram for normal bromination (e.g. propylene).
- Answer: for adamantylideneadamantane, the bromonium ion (with Br⁻ or Br3⁻) sits in a deep well. The backside approach is blocked by the two adamantyl cages, so the barrier from the bromonium ion to the dibromide is very high and no product forms. For propene, the bromonium ion is a high-energy intermediate with a small barrier to anti attack by Br⁻, and the dibromide product lies downhill [H].
- Concept: bromonium-ion mechanism; steric hindrance to the SN2-like opening step.

### Problem 9.84 — book p. 459 (PDF p-27) — footnote 4 → J. Org. Chem. 1984, 49, 945–947
- Compound: diisopinocampheylborane (Ipc2BH), a chiral hydroborating agent made from BH3 and 2 equiv α-pinene (`CC1=CCC2CC1C2(C)C`, single enantiomer).
- Reagents: α-pinene + BH3·THF (0.5 equiv) → Ipc2BH (single diastereomer/enantiomer).
- Asked: explain why only one stereoisomer of Ipc2BH forms.
- Answer: hydroboration is syn and concerted. Boron adds to the less substituted alkene carbon, anti-Markovnikov. B–H adds only to the face of the alkene opposite the C6 gem-dimethyl bridge (the less hindered face). Because the α-pinene is enantiopure, a single isomer forms. The second hydroboration (by IpcBH2) also occurs from the same face [H].
- Concept: steric face selectivity; substrate-controlled asymmetric hydroboration.

### Problem 9.85 — book p. 460 (PDF p-28) — footnote 5 → Angew. Chem. Int. Ed. 1994, 33, 2187–2190 (zaragozic acid A synthesis)
- Natural product: zaragozic acid A (squalene synthase inhibitor, cholesterol-lowering).
- Starting material 1: an allylic alcohol, (2-hydroxyethylidene)-γ-butyrolactone. Its α-carbon is a spiro center shared with a 2,2-dimethyl-1,3-dioxolane (acetonide) that carries CH2OSEM (SEM = CH2OCH2CH2Si(CH3)3). Constitution SMILES `OCC=C1COC(=O)C12OC(C)(C)OC2COCOCC[Si](C)(C)C`; alkene geometry and stereo not encoded [M].
- Reagents: OsO4 (cat.), NMO → triol 2 (diastereoselective), which rapidly rearranges (translactonization) to lactone 3.
- What happens: 2 = syn-dihydroxylated triol (a tertiary OH at the former ring alkene carbon, a secondary OH plus CH2OH on the side chain). In 3, the lactone carbonyl has migrated to the new secondary OH, forming a new γ-lactone that bears CH2OH and a quaternary C(OH)CH2OH, and releasing the former ring CH2OH.
- Asked: explain the diastereoselectivity (syn addition from the top π face, not the bottom), using a molecular model.
- Answer: the bottom face of the exocyclic alkene is shielded by the spiro-fused acetonide (its gem-dimethyl/ring O) and the CH2OSEM substituent, so OsO4 approaches from the open top face [M]. Optional: mechanism of translactonization (intramolecular transesterification via a tetrahedral intermediate) [H].
- Concept: OsO4 syn dihydroxylation; steric face selectivity in a rigid spiro system.

### Problem 9.86 — book p. 460 (PDF p-28) — footnote 6 → J. Am. Chem. Soc. 1980, 102, 2118–2120 (monensin synthesis; book says "W. S. Still")
- Natural product: monensin (antibiotic, Streptomyces cinnamonensis).
- Starting material 1: (Z)-C5=C6 unsaturated carboxylic acid with C2-Me and C4-Me stereocenters. The chain end carries a CH(CH3) attached to a 2,2-dimethyl-1,3-dioxolan-4-yl (acetonide). SMILES (no stereocenters) `CC(CC(C)C(=O)O)/C=C\CC(C)C1COC(C)(C)O1`. The alkene reads as Z from the drawing [M].
- Reagents: NaHCO3, I2 (iodolactonization) → δ-lactone 2. Ring O is bonded to C5 and I to C6; only the (5S,6S) diastereomer forms, and (5R,6R) is not observed. Product constitution `CC1CC(C)C(=O)OC1C(I)CC(C)C1COC(C)(C)O1`.
- Asked: (a) mechanism (iodonium formation, then anti attack of carboxylate at C5, 6-membered ring); (b) explain the diastereoselectivity from the conformation of 1 using a C4–C5 Newman projection.
- Answer: the lowest-energy conformer about C4–C5 avoids A(1,3) strain between the C4 substituents and the cis C6 substituent of the Z-alkene, so C4–H eclipses the alkene. I⁺ then adds to the less hindered face, and carboxylate attacks anti, giving 5S,6S [M].
- Concept: halolactonization, anti addition, allylic (A1,3) strain–controlled diastereoselectivity.

### Problem 9.87 (도전 문제) — book p. 460 — footnote 7 → J. Am. Chem. Soc. 1989, 111, 1414–1418
- Data: relative rates of oxymercuration with Hg(OAc)2. CH2=C(CH3)CH2CH2CH3 (1) 1000; CH2=CHCH2CH2CH2CH3 (2) 100; CH2=CHCH2OMe (3) 31.8; (CH3)CH=C(CH3)2 (2-methyl-2-butene, 4) 25.8; CH2=CHCH2Cl (5) 2.4.
- Asked: explain the trends in structural terms.
- Answer: electrophilic addition goes through a mercurinium ion with carbocation character. 1,1-Disubstituted 1 > monosubstituted 2 (more alkyl donation stabilizes the cationic TS). Allylic OMe (3) and Cl (5) are inductively electron-withdrawing and slow the reaction, Cl more strongly. The trisubstituted 4 is slower than 1 despite being more electron-rich because steric hindrance at the bulky Hg(OAc)2 approach dominates [M–H].

### Problem 9.88 (도전 문제) — book p. 460 — footnote 8 → J. Am. Chem. Soc. 1989, 111, 1414–1418
- Data: relative rates of hydroboration with 9-BBN. CH2=CHOBu (1) 1615; CH2=CHBu (2) 100; CH2=CHCH2OMe (3) 32.5; CH2=CHOAc (4) 22.8; CH2=CHCH2OAc (5) 21.9; CH2=CHCH2CN (6) 5.9; CH2=CHCH2Cl (7) 4.0; cis-2-butene (8) 0.95; trans-CH3CH2CH=CHCl (9) 0.003.
- Asked: (a) explain 1–5; (b) 5, 6, 7; (c) 2, 8, 9.
- Answers: (a) vinyl ether 1 is the fastest because O donates by resonance and makes the π bond more nucleophilic (boron is electrophilic). The vinyl ester 4 donates much less because the O lone pair is delocalized into C=O. Allylic OMe (3) and OAc (5) are inductively withdrawing, so slower than 2. (b) The rate falls with the inductive withdrawal of the allylic group: OAc < CN ≈ Cl (Cl and CN strongest). (c) Internal disubstituted 8 is ~100× slower than terminal 2 because bulky 9-BBN is very sensitive to steric hindrance. 9 (internal plus a vinylic Cl that withdraws inductively) is slowest [M].

### Problem 9.89 (도전 문제) — book p. 460 — footnote 9 → Org. Lett. 2012, 14, 2929–2931 (heliol synthesis)
- Starting material: 2-(2-hydroxy-6-methylhept-6-en-2-yl)phenol, `C=C(C)CCCC(C)(O)c1ccccc1O` [H].
- Reagents: H3O⁺, CH3CN → bridged benzoxa-bicyclic (heliol core) + enantiomer (racemic).
- Product: bicyclo[3.3.1] bridged ether fused to benzene, with two bridgehead methyls, `CC12CCCC(C)(C1)Oc1ccccc12` (C14H18O) [M–H].
- Asked: mechanism.
- Answer: protonate the tertiary benzylic OH and lose water to give a tertiary benzylic cation. The 1,1-disubstituted alkene attacks it intramolecularly (terminal CH2 forms a new C–C bond, closing a 6-membered ring) to give a new tertiary cation. The phenol O traps that cation to form the O-bridge, and deprotonation finishes. Racemic because the cation is planar and achiral [H].
- Concept: carbocation formation, cation–olefin cyclization, intramolecular ether formation.

### Problem 9.90 (도전 문제) — book p. 460 — footnote 10 → Tetrahedron 1958, 2, 1–57 (R. B. Woodward reserpine)
- Natural product: reserpine (Rauwolfia serpentina).
- Starting material 1: a cis-fused tricyclic γ-lactone (decalin-type) with an allylic secondary OH on a cyclohexene ring and a second C=C in the other ring. SMILES uncertain.
- Reagents: 1) Br2; 2) NaOMe → short-lived intermediate 2 (an ether-bridged α,β-unsaturated lactone), which undergoes Michael addition of MeO⁻ to give 3 (OMe added β to the lactone carbonyl).
- Asked: mechanism for 1 → 2.
- Answer: Br2 adds to the ring alkene near the OH to form a bromonium ion. The OH attacks intramolecularly, anti, to form the cyclic ether (bromoetherification), leaving Br on the adjacent carbon. NaOMe deprotonates the acidic lactone α-C–H, and E2 (or E1cb) eliminates HBr to give the conjugated α,β-unsaturated lactone 2. MeO⁻ then adds 1,4 to give 3 [M].
- Concept: bromonium ion with an intramolecular O-nucleophile; base-induced elimination; conjugate addition.

(Other problems in 9.53–9.81, e.g. 9.76 muscalure and 9.80 iodolactonization, have no literature footnote.)

## Chapter 10 — 알카인 (Alkynes) — PDF p-30..p-37, book pp. 489–496

### Summary page (반응의 복습, book p. 489, PDF p-30)
1. Elimination: geminal or vicinal dihalide + excess NaNH2; H2O → terminal alkyne (double E2).
2. Hydrohalogenation, excess HX → geminal dihalide (Markovnikov).
3. HX (1 equiv) → vinyl halide (Markovnikov).
4. Acid-catalyzed hydration: H2SO4, H2O, HgSO4 → methyl ketone (via enol, then tautomerization).
5. Hydroboration–oxidation: 1) R2BH (disiamylborane/9-BBN); 2) H2O2, NaOH → aldehyde (anti-Markovnikov enol, then tautomerization).
6. Halogenation, X2 (1 equiv), CCl4 → (E)-1,2-dihaloalkene.
7. Excess X2, CCl4 → 1,1,2,2-tetrahaloalkane.
8. Ozonolysis: 1) O3; 2) H2O → carboxylic acids (terminal carbon → CO2).
9. Alkylation: 1) NaNH2; 2) RX (methyl/1°) → internal alkyne.
10. Dissolving-metal reduction: Na, NH3(l) → trans alkene (radical anion; fishhook arrows).
11. Hydrogenation: H2, Pt → alkane.
12. Poisoned catalyst: H2, Lindlar catalyst (or Ni2B) → cis alkene.

Concepts: sp C, acetylide pKa ~25 vs H2O 15.7 (NaNH2 or NaH needed); keto–enol tautomerization under acid/base; vinyl cation vs termolecular HX addition; HBr/ROOR anti-Markovnikov; acetylene double alkylation; alkene → alkyne via Br2 then excess NaNH2.

### Problem 10.69 — book p. 494 (PDF p-35) — footnote 1 → Org. Lett. 2003, 5, 4017–4020
- Topic: synthesis of α,α-disubstituted α-amino acids (Ch. 25 targets).
- Starting material: a quaternary carbon bearing N(Boc)2, a terminal ethynyl (C≡CH), CH2–O–CH2–(3,4-dimethoxyphenyl) (wedge), and CH2–O–TBS (hash). Model SMILES with Boc and TBS in full: `C#CC(N(C(=O)OC(C)(C)C)C(=O)OC(C)(C)C)(COCc1ccc(OC)c(OC)c1)CO[Si](C)(C)C(C)(C)C`, stereo omitted [M].
- Product: the C≡CH becomes CH2CH2CH2–O–CH2CH2–OCH3 (three CH2 groups, then the ether; verified at 600-dpi zoom). Everything else is unchanged.
- Asked: (a) reagents; (b) configuration at the chiral center in the starting material and product.
- Answer: (a) 1) NaNH2 (or n-BuLi), to form the acetylide; 2) CH3OCH2CH2OCH2Cl (MEM chloride) for SN2 alkylation, giving C≡C–CH2–O–CH2CH2OMe; 3) H2, Pt (or Pd/C) to reduce the alkyne fully to the alkane. This accounts for the extra carbon [M–H]. (b) CIP priority: N > CH2OTBS (O–Si beats O–C) > CH2O–CH2Ar > the C≡CH or CH2CH2 chain. The priorities don't change, so both are S as drawn (RDKit on the drawn wedge/hash: N up-left, alkyne up-right, CH2OAr wedge, CH2OTBS hash) [M].
- Concept: acetylide alkylation followed by complete hydrogenation; CIP ranking.

### Problem 10.70 — book p. 494 (PDF p-35) — footnote 2 → J. Org. Chem. 1959, 24, 840–842
- Reaction: (CH3O)2SO2 (1 mol) + HC≡CNa (2 mol) → 2 mol propyne (CH3C≡CH) + ?
- Asked: (a) the ionic inorganic byproduct and its mechanism; (b) why 2-butyne is a byproduct, consistent with acetylene being observed; (c) products with diethyl sulfate.
- Answer: (a) Na2SO4. The first SN2 transfers a methyl and gives CH3OSO3⁻Na⁺. Methyl sulfate is still an alkylating agent and methylates the second acetylide, leaving SO4²⁻ (2 Na⁺) [H]. (b) Acetylide (a stronger base) deprotonates the propyne product (acid–base exchange). This gives acetylene and CH3C≡C⁻, which is methylated to CH3C≡CCH3. Acetylene shows up among the products [H]. (c) Main product 1-butyne (2 mol), plus Na2SO4; byproducts 3-hexyne and acetylene [H].

### Problem 10.71 — book pp. 494–495 (PDF p-35/36) — footnote 3 → J. Am. Chem. Soc. 2007, 129, 8968–8969 (salvinorin A synthesis)
- Natural product: salvinorin A (Salvia divinorum, κ-opioid agonist, hallucinogen).
- Starting material 1: 1-(furan-3-yl)but-2-yn-1-ol, `CC#CC(O)c1ccoc1` [H].
- Reagents: 1) NaNH2; 2) H2O → 2: 1-(furan-3-yl)but-3-yn-1-ol, `C#CCC(O)c1ccoc1` [H].
- Asked: mechanism (hint: problem 10.8).
- Answer: alkyne "zipper" isomerization. Repeated deprotonation and reprotonation moves the triple bond through an allene intermediate (internal alkyne → allene → terminal alkyne). Irreversible deprotonation of the terminal alkyne to the acetylide (pKa ~25, NH3 ~38) drives the equilibrium. H2O workup then protonates the acetylide. The alkoxide is also formed and is reprotonated on workup [H].

### Problem 10.72 — book p. 495 (PDF p-36) — footnote 4 → Tetrahedron Lett. 1996, 37, 1571–1574
- Starting material: cyclodeca-1,6-diyne, `C1CC#CCCCC#CC1` [H].
- Reagent: I2 → diiododiene (94%), a transannular cyclization. Product: bicyclic (two fused 6-membered rings), conjugated diene with two vinyl iodides, `IC1=C2CCCC(I)=C2CCC1` (C10H12I2) [M–H].
- Asked: (a) draw the bridged iodonium ion (iodirenium) from attack of the alkyne on I2; (b) draw the vinyl carbocation that would form if the other alkyne attacked as a nucleophile, and explain why it is high in energy (sp-hybridized vinyl cation, positive charge on an electronegative sp carbon, little stabilization); (c) draw the concerted step: the second alkyne attacks the iodonium while iodide attacks the developing cation (iodonium opening and transannular C–C bond formation together, avoiding a free vinyl cation), so the full mechanism has two steps [M–H].

### Problem 10.73 — book p. 495 (PDF p-36) — footnote 5 → Acc. Chem. Res. 1988, 21, 442–449 (stable simple enols)
- Compounds: 1 = diphenylacetaldehyde, `O=CC(c1ccccc1)c1ccccc1` (9.1% enol); 2 = dimesitylacetaldehyde, `O=CC(c1c(C)cc(C)cc1C)c1c(C)cc(C)cc1C` (95% enol). Compare (CH3)2CHCHO at 0.014% enol.
- Asked: (a) hybridization and bond angles at the carbon carrying both rings (sp3, ~109.5°); (b) the enol of 1 and the same for that carbon in the enol (sp2, ~120°); (c) whether the angle increases or decreases on enolization, and why sterics favor the enol; (d) why enol 2 is more favored than enol 1.
- Answer: on enolization the angle widens from 109.5° to 120°, which relieves steric repulsion between the two bulky aryls. The enol C=C is also conjugated with the aryl rings. Mesityl groups (ortho-methyls) are much bulkier, so relief of steric strain in 2 is larger and the keto form of 2 is destabilized [H].

### Problem 10.74 — book p. 495 (PDF p-36) — footnote 6 → J. Am. Chem. Soc. 1966, 88, 4525–4526
- Starting material 1: 1,2-bis(phenylethynyl)benzene, `C(#Cc1ccccc1)c1ccccc1C#Cc1ccccc1` [H].
- Reactions:
  - H2SO4, H2O → 3-benzoyl-2-phenyl-1H-indene, `O=C(c1ccccc1)C1=C(c2ccccc2)Cc2ccccc21` [H].
  - Br2 → 3-bromo-1-[bromo(phenyl)methylene]-2-phenyl-1H-indene, `BrC(c1ccccc1)=C1C(c2ccccc2)=C(Br)c2ccccc21` (exocyclic alkene). As drawn, the exocyclic Br points toward the benzo ring and that carbon's Ph points toward C2 [M].
- Asked: arrow-pushing mechanisms for both, and an explanation of the geometry of the exocyclic alkene in the bromination product.
- Answer:
  - Hydration: H⁺ adds to the arene-bound alkyne carbon (Ca), giving a Ph-stabilized vinyl cation at Cb. The other alkyne's arene-bound carbon Cc attacks Cb (5-membered ring closure), giving a new Ph-stabilized vinyl cation at Cd. Water attacks Cd, then deprotonation gives the enol, which tautomerizes to the ketone. A final acid-catalyzed tautomerization/H shift gives the conjugated indene [M–H].
  - Bromination: Br⁺ adds to Ca (bromonium or vinyl cation), and the same cascade gives an exocyclic vinyl cation at Cd. Its empty p orbital lies in the ring plane. Br⁻ attacks in plane from the less hindered side, away from the C2-phenyl and toward the benzo/peri H side, which sets the geometry [M].

### Problem 10.75 (도전 문제) — book p. 495 (PDF p-36) — footnote 7 → J. Am. Chem. Soc. 2008, 130, 6281–6287 (roquefortine C)
- Natural product: roquefortine C (Penicillium roqueforti, blue cheese; antibacterial). Indoline-fused diketopiperazine with a reverse-prenyl (C(CH3)2CH=CH2) group, a dehydrohistidine exocyclic alkylidene, and an imidazole. It exists as two imidazole tautomers: the 1H (Nτ–H) form on the left and the 3H (Nπ–H, NH adjacent to the vinyl-substituted carbon) form on the right. Full SMILES uncertain (complex stereo).
- Asked: (a) base-catalyzed tautomerization mechanism; (b) acid-catalyzed mechanism; (c) which tautomer is more stable, and why.
- Answer: (a) base removes the imidazole N–H to give an imidazolide anion (resonance delocalized over both N), which is reprotonated at the other N [H]. (b) Acid protonates the basic imidazole sp2 N to give an imidazolium cation, and the original N–H is then deprotonated [H]. (c) Likely the right-hand (3H) tautomer: its N–H can form an intramolecular hydrogen bond to the nearby diketopiperazine C=O, and it is conjugated through the exocyclic alkene. The paper reports a solvent-dependent mixture, and the H-bonded tautomer is favored in nonpolar media [L–M; check against the paper].

### Problem 10.76 (도전 문제) — book pp. 495–496 (PDF p-36/37) — footnote 8 → Tetrahedron Lett. 2001, 42, 7277–7280
- Topic: N-reverse-prenylated indoles (N-1,1-dimethyl-2-propenyl), antitumor/antifungal.
- Starting material 1: indoline, `C1Cc2ccccc2N1`. Reagents: 2-methylbut-3-yn-2-yl acetate (`CC(C)(C#C)OC(C)=O`), CuCl (cat.), i-Pr2NEt → 2 (C13H15N), then → 3 = N-(2-methylbut-3-en-2-yl)indoline, `CC(C)(C=C)N1CCc2ccccc21`.
- Asked: (a) structure of 2 and mechanism, ignoring the catalyst; (b) reagents for 2 → 3.
- Answer: (a) 2 = N-(2-methylbut-3-yn-2-yl)indoline, `CC(C)(C#C)N1CCc2ccccc21` (C13H15N, checked). Mechanism treated as SN1: the acetate leaves to give a resonance-stabilized tertiary propargylic cation (with an allenyl resonance form). The indoline N attacks the tertiary carbon, and i-Pr2NEt removes N–H [H]. (b) H2, Lindlar catalyst (or Ni2B/P-2) → terminal alkene [H].

### Problem 10.77 (도전 문제) — book p. 496 (PDF p-37) — footnote 9 → J. Phys. Chem. A 2007, 111, 3513–3518
- Compound: 3-heptyne, `CCCC#CCC`, treated as a "stretched" pentane with the alkyne inserted between pentane C2 and C3. Pentane's lowest conformers are anti-anti and anti-gauche.
- Finding: in 3-heptyne's two most stable conformers, C1 and C6 are nearly eclipsed (sighting down the alkyne). C4/C7 are anti about C5–C6 in one conformer and gauche in the other.
- Asked: (a) wedge-dash drawings of pentane's anti-anti and anti-gauche conformers; (b) the 3-heptyne analog of anti-anti; (c) Newman projection sighted down the C≡C axis (C2···C5) showing C1 eclipsing C6, which is acceptable because the 4.1 Å separation removes steric strain and there is a slight dispersion attraction; (d) Newman projections about C5–C6 with C4 and C7 anti vs gauche [M–H].

### Problem 10.78 (도전 문제) — book p. 496 (PDF p-37) — footnote 10 → J. Am. Chem. Soc. 1976, 98, 3295–3300
- Substrates: Ph–C≡C–R (1a R = Me, `CC#Cc1ccccc1`; 1b Et; 1c i-Pr; 1d t-Bu) + HCl → PhC(Cl)=CHR, E:Z = 70:30, 80:20, 95:5, 100:0.
- As drawn, E means Cl trans to R (R cis to Ph).
- Asked: (a) the vinyl cation from protonation of a phenyl-substituted acetylene, and the regioselectivity; (b) the stereoselectivity (E:Z) from transition-state stability during capture by chloride.
- Answer: (a) H⁺ adds to the R-bearing carbon, giving a linear sp vinyl cation Ph–C⁺=CHR stabilized by resonance with phenyl, so Cl ends up on the Ph-bearing carbon [H]. (b) The empty p orbital lies in the plane of the C2 substituents (H and R). Cl⁻ approaches in plane from the H side (less hindered) rather than the R side, giving Cl cis to H and trans to R (E). E-selectivity rises as R gets bulkier (Me < Et < i-Pr < t-Bu) [H].

## Chapter 11 — 라디칼 반응 (Radical Reactions) — PDF p-38..p-45, book pp. 533–540

### Summary (반응의 복습, book p. 533; 개념 pp. 533–534, PDF p-38/39)
Radical reactions useful in synthesis:
- Alkane bromination: Br2, hν → Br at the most substituted carbon (3° ≫ 2° > 1°; much more selective than Cl2).
- Anti-Markovnikov HBr addition: HBr, ROOR → Br on the less substituted carbon (radical chain).
- Allylic bromination: NBS, hν (or heat/peroxide) → allylic bromide; the allylic radical can give two regioisomers.
- Also: radical stability 3° > 2° > 1° (allylic/benzylic resonance stabilized; vinylic not). Six arrow patterns: homolysis, addition to π, H abstraction, halogen abstraction, elimination, coupling. Initiation / propagation / termination. Initiators: peroxides, acyl peroxides, AIBN. Inhibitors: O2, hydroquinone. A new stereocenter formed at a radical carbon → racemic. Ozone/CFCs. Autoxidation → hydroperoxides. Antioxidants (BHT, BHA, vitamins E, C). Radical polymerization (PE, PVC), cracking/hydrocracking. Halogenation installs a functional group on an alkane.

### 참고문헌 (book p. 540, PDF p-45), exact text

### Problem 11.49 — book p. 538 (PDF p-43) — footnote 1 → J. Am. Chem. Soc. 2012, 134, 6916–6919 (footnote is on "특별한 효소")
- Reaction (thiol–ene coupling): R–CH=CH2 + H–S–R′, radical initiator (Δ or hν) → R–CH2–CH2–S–R′ (anti-Markovnikov).
- Asked: (a) mechanism with initiation and propagation; (b) the product expected when a protein bearing an N-allyl amide (protein–C(=O)NH–CH2CH=CH2) is thiol–ene coupled with a protein thiol (protein–SH, cysteine).
- Answer: (a) Initiation: the initiator radical abstracts H from R′S–H to give R′S•. Propagation: (1) R′S• adds to the terminal CH2, giving the more stable 2° radical; (2) that radical abstracts H from R′SH, giving the product and regenerating R′S• [H]. (b) Protein–C(=O)NH–CH2CH2CH2–S–protein, a thioether link made by anti-Markovnikov addition [H].

### Problem 11.50 — book p. 538 (PDF p-43) — footnote 2 → J. Phys. Chem. A 2008, 112, 7930–7938
- Starting material: 1-bromopropane, `BrCCC` (industrial solvent, CFC replacement). Atmospheric oxidation → bromoacetone, `BrCC(C)=O` (main product) + others.
- Asked: mechanism with 4 given steps.
- Answer: (1) HO• abstracts the C2–H (secondary) to give BrCH2C•HCH3 + H2O. (2) O2 couples to give the peroxy radical BrCH2CH(OO•)CH3. (3) NO abstracts an O atom to give NO2 + the alkoxy radical BrCH2CH(O•)CH3. (4) O2 abstracts the C2–H α to O•, giving BrCH2C(=O)CH3 + HOO• [H].

### Problem 11.51 — book p. 538 (PDF p-43) — footnote 3 → J. Org. Chem. 2009, 74, 5025–5031
- Compound: resveratrol (trans-3,5,4′-trihydroxystilbene; grapes, mulberry, peanuts; antioxidant), `Oc1ccc(/C=C/c2cc(O)cc(O)c2)cc1`.
- Reaction: alkoxy radical RO• abstracts a phenolic H.
- Asked: which H is abstracted preferentially, and why.
- Answer: the 4′-OH (para to the vinyl group). The resulting phenoxyl radical is delocalized through the para position onto the C=C and into the other ring (extended conjugation; many resonance forms). Radicals from the 3- or 5-OH (meta) cannot delocalize onto the alkene [H].

### Problem 11.52 — book p. 538 (PDF p-43) — footnote 4 → J. Am. Chem. Soc. 2000, 122, 3367–3374
- Concept: resonance of a radical next to an atom with a lone pair (P, S can expand the octet).
- Reaction: cinnamyl arenesulfenate PhCH=CHCH2–O–S–Ar, heat → cinnamyl sulfoxide PhCH=CHCH2–S(=O)–Ar. Reported to go by C–O homolysis followed by radical recombination (two steps).
- Asked: (a) mechanism; (b) why C–O breaks first rather than O–S.
- Answer: (a) C–O homolysis gives the cinnamyl radical (allylic, conjugated with Ph) + the arylsulfinyl radical ArS–O• ↔ ArS•=O (delocalized over O and S). Recombination at S gives the sulfoxide [M–H]. (b) C–O cleavage gives two resonance-stabilized radicals (allylic/benzylic-type cinnamyl; sulfinyl delocalized onto S through the adjacent lone pair). O–S cleavage would give an unstabilized alkoxyl radical, so C–O is the weaker bond here [M–H].

### Problem 11.53 — book p. 539 (PDF p-44) — footnote 5 → J. Am. Chem. Soc. 2006, 128, 6310–6311 (oseltamivir synthesis)
- Target: oseltamivir (Tamiflu, anti-influenza). 2 is a key intermediate.
- Starting material 1: bicyclic iodolactam, a 6-azabicyclo[3.2.1]octan-7-one with N–R. I sits on the ring carbon next to the N-bearing bridgehead (I wedge). Constitution `IC1CCC2CC1N(R)C2=O` (R generic; shown with R = CH3 in the RDKit check) [M].
- Reagents: 1) t-BuOK; 2) NBS, hν → 2: allylic bromide `BrC1C=CC2CC1N(R)C2=O`. Br is on the same carbon and the same face (wedge) as the I was; the C=C has moved to the next two carbons, away from the bridgehead [M].
- Asked: mechanism and stereochemical outcome.
- Answer: E2 with t-BuOK removes the anti-periplanar H from the CH2 away from the bridgehead (Bredt's rule: no alkene to the bridgehead), giving the alkene. NBS/hν then abstracts the allylic H at the CH2 next to the other bridgehead (the bridgehead C–H is not usable). The resulting allylic radical is delocalized, and Br2 delivers Br to the other allylic terminus, moving the alkene. Br adds from the less hindered (exo) face, away from the lactam bridge, so a single diastereomer forms [M].

### Problem 11.54 (도전 문제) — book p. 539 (PDF p-44) — footnote 6 → J. Am. Chem. Soc. 1986, 108, 1708–1709
- Starting radical: •C(CN)(CO2Et)–CH2CH2–(cycloocta-2,6-dien-1-yl), modeled as `[C](C#N)(C(=O)OCC)CCC1C=CCCC=CC1`. Diene positions inferred from the products [L–M].
- Products: two tricyclic radicals, each formed by two new ring closures. (i) A linear triquinane radical (three fused cyclopentanes; C(CO2Et)(CN) in the terminal ring; radical in the far ring). (ii) A bridged tricycle (5-ring + bicyclic containing C(CN)(CO2Et)) with the radical in the cyclopentane.
- Asked: mechanisms for each two-ring formation.
- Answer: (i) 5-exo-trig: the α-cyano-ester radical attacks ring C2 (forming a cyclopentane with CH2CH2 and C1), leaving a radical at C3. Transannular C3→C7 addition then forms the bicyclo[3.3.0]octane, leaving a radical at C6 (linear triquinane) [M]. (ii) 6-endo addition at C3 first (radical at C2), then transannular C2→C6, leaving a radical at C7 (bridged product) [L].

### Problem 11.55 (도전 문제) — book p. 539 (PDF p-44) — footnote 7 → Tetrahedron Lett. 2006, 47, 2115–2118
- Starting material: bisphenyl carbodiazone PhN=N–C(=O)–N=NPh, `O=C(N=Nc1ccccc1)N=Nc1ccccc1`.
- Conditions: hν (350 nm), TEMPO → three products: PhN=N–C(=O)–O–TMP (`O=C(N=Nc1ccccc1)ON1C(C)(C)CCCC1(C)C`), Ph–O–TMP (`CC1(C)CCCC(C)(C)N1Oc1ccccc1`), and N2.
- Asked: a mechanism explaining all three products.
- Answer: homolysis of one C–N bond gives PhN=N–C•(=O) (acyl-type radical) + PhN=N•. PhN=N• loses N2 to give Ph•. TEMPO traps Ph• (O–C coupling) and traps the acyl radical, giving the carbamoyl-type ester [H].

### Problem 11.56 (도전 문제) — book p. 539 (PDF p-44) — footnote 8 → J. Am. Chem. Soc. 2001, 123, 1842–1847
- Reaction: cubane (1, `C12C3C4C1C5C2C3C45`) + I–Z (2a I2: no reaction; 2b CI4) → iodocubane (3, `IC12C3C4C1C5C2C3C45`) + H–Z (4a HI; 4b HCI3, iodoform). Initiated by •CI3.
- Asked: (a) propagation cycle; (b) three possible termination steps; (c) given BDE(I–CI3) = 192 and BDE(H–CI3) = 423 kJ/mol, why 2b works and 2a does not.
- Answer: (a) •CI3 + cubane-H → HCI3 + cubyl•; cubyl• + CI4 → cubyl–I + •CI3 [H]. (b) cubyl• + •CI3 → cubyl–CI3; 2 •CI3 → I3C–CI3; 2 cubyl• → bicubyl [H]. (c) H abstraction by I• forms H–I (~297 kJ/mol), while the cubane C–H costs ~400+ kJ/mol, so it is strongly endothermic. •CI3 forms H–CI3 (423 kJ/mol), so H abstraction is roughly thermoneutral or favorable. The weak I–CI3 bond (192) makes iodine transfer to cubyl• exothermic. Both propagation steps are thermodynamically feasible only with CI4 [H].

### Problem 11.57 (도전 문제) — book p. 539 (PDF p-44) — footnote 9 → Tetrahedron Lett. 2002, 43, 8587–8590 (paclitaxel fragment)
- (a) Kharasch reaction: R–CH=CH2 + CCl4, (PhCO2)2, heat → R–CH(Cl)–CH2–CCl3.
  - Answer: initiation: PhCO2–O2CPh → 2 PhCO2•, then Ph• + CO2; Ph• (or PhCO2•) abstracts Cl from CCl4 to give •CCl3. Propagation: •CCl3 adds to the terminal CH2 (less hindered, gives the 2° radical); the radical abstracts Cl from CCl4 to give the product and regenerate •CCl3. Regiochemistry follows the more stable radical intermediate [H].
- (b) Substrate: a furanose acetonide (1,2-O-isopropylidene) with a C3 quaternary carbinol bearing a vinyl group, and C4 bearing CH2–CH(OH)–C(CH3)=CH2. SMILES `C=CC1(O)C2OC(C)(C)OC2OC1CC(O)C(C)=C` (C14H22O5) [M–H].
  - Conditions: CCl4, (PhCOO)2, heat → cyclohexane fused to the furanose: ring C(CH3)(CH2CCl3)–CH(OH)–CH2–CH(O)–C(OH)–CH(CH2Cl). SMILES `OC1CC2OC3OC(C)(C)OC3C2(O)C(CCl)C1(C)CC(Cl)(Cl)Cl` (C15H22Cl4O5 = SM + CCl4, checked) [M–H].
  - Answer: •CCl3 adds to the =CH2 of the isopropenyl group, giving a 3° radical. 6-exo-trig cyclization onto the internal CH of the vinyl group gives a primary CH2• radical, which abstracts Cl from CCl4 (CH2Cl) and regenerates •CCl3 [H].

### Problem 11.58 (도전 문제) — book p. 540 (PDF p-45) — footnote 10 → J. Am. Chem. Soc. 2009, 131, 653–661
- Reaction: phenylacetylene (`C#Cc1ccccc1`) + nitrosobenzene (`O=Nc1ccccc1`), heat → diradical Ph–C•=CH–N(O•)–Ph (vinyl radical + nitroxide) → N-hydroxy-3-phenylindole, `ON1C=C(c2ccccc2)c2ccccc21` [H].
- Asked: stepwise mechanism from the diradical to the product.
- Answer: the vinyl radical (carbon bearing Ph) adds to the ortho carbon of the N-phenyl ring (5-membered ring closure), giving a cyclohexadienyl radical alongside the nitroxide. Transfer of the ring ipso/ortho H (rearomatization; intramolecular H-atom transfer, or tautomerization) to the nitroxide O gives the aromatic indole N–OH [M–H].

## Chapter 12 — 합성 (Synthesis) — PDF p-46..p-51, book pp. 561–566 (bonus: in my page range although the file is labeled 7–11)

### Summary (개념과 용어의 복습 p. 561 + 학습능력 p. 562, PDF p-46/47)
- Chapter-opening box: Taxol, synthesized in 1994 by Holton (Florida State) and Nicolaou (Scripps). Semisynthesis from 10-deacetylbaccatin III (Taxus baccata needles); Taxotere (docetaxel).
- Moving a halogen: elimination then addition (e.g., NaOEt; then HBr Markovnikov or HBr/ROOR).
- Moving a π bond: addition then elimination (HBr; then t-BuOK or NaOEt).
- Alkane ⇄ alkene ⇄ alkyne interconversion:
  - alkyne → cis-alkene: H2/Lindlar; alkyne → trans-alkene: Na/NH3.
  - alkene → alkane: H2/Pt.
  - alkane → alkene: 1) Br2, hν; 2) NaOEt.
  - alkene → alkyne: 1) Br2/CCl4; 2) excess NaNH2; 3) H2O.
- C–C bond formation: acetylide + alkyl halide. C–C cleavage: ozonolysis (1) O3; 2) DMS).
- Two questions for any synthesis problem: (1) does the carbon skeleton change? (2) does the identity or position of the functional group change? Retrosynthetic analysis; green chemistry; multiple correct answers.
- Comprehensive problems 12.27/12.28 introduce acetylide + epoxide → homopropargylic alcohol, and acetylide + ketone/aldehyde → propargylic alcohol (protonation as a separate step).

### 참고문헌 (book p. 566, PDF p-51), exact text
1. J. Agric. Food Chem. 2003, 51, 4344–4348.
2. Agric. Biol. Chem. 1988, 52, 1053–1055.
3. J. Agric. Food Chem. 2005, 53, 7204–7211.
4. J. Agric. Food Chem. 1983, 31, 1287–1292.
5. J. Nat. Prod. 2012, 75, 1765–1776.
6. Nat. Prod. Rep. 2012, 29, 1288–1303.
7. J. Agric. Food Chem. 2002, 50, 3401–3404.
8. Actinomycetologica 2009, 23, 27–33.
9. J. Agric. Food Chem. 2009, 57, 3818–3830.
10. Tetrahedron 1977, 33, 2447–2450.

### Problem 12.32 — book p. 564 (PDF p-49) — footnote 1 → J. Agric. Food Chem. 2003, 51, 4344–4348
- Target: 3-phenylpropyl acetate, `CC(=O)OCCCc1ccccc1` (one of 27 volatiles in Sri Lankan endemic cinnamon). Carbon sources: toluene (`Cc1ccccc1`) plus two-carbon reactants.
- Proposed route [M]:
  1. Toluene + NBS, hν → benzyl bromide.
  2. + HC≡CNa → 3-phenylpropyne.
  3. H2, Lindlar → allylbenzene.
  4. HBr, ROOR → 1-bromo-3-phenylpropane.
  5. CH3CO2⁻Na⁺ (SN2) → ester.
- Alternative for the last steps: hydroboration–oxidation → 3-phenyl-1-propanol, then acetylation.

### Problem 12.33 — book p. 565 (PDF p-50) — footnote 2 → Agric. Biol. Chem. 1988, 52, 1053–1055
- Target: 2,5-hexanedione, `CC(=O)CCC(C)=O` (one of 33 volatiles of kakkon, Pueraria root). Starting material: 3,4-dimethylcyclobutene, `CC1C=CC1C`.
- Key point: direct ozonolysis would give 2,3-dimethylsuccinaldehyde, so the C=C must first move to the methyl-bearing carbons.
- Route [M–H]:
  1. H2, Pt → 1,2-dimethylcyclobutane.
  2. Br2, hν → 1-bromo-1,2-dimethylcyclobutane (3°).
  3. NaOEt (Zaitsev) → 1,2-dimethylcyclobutene.
  4. O3; DMS → 2,5-hexanedione.

### Problem 12.34 — book p. 565 (PDF p-50) — footnote 3 → J. Agric. Food Chem. 2005, 53, 7204–7211
- Target: 1-penten-3-ol, `C=CC(O)CC` (volatile of indoor- vs outdoor-raised quail feed). Acetylene is the only carbon source; use the acetylide + carbonyl reaction of 12.28.
- Route [M–H]:
  1. Make propanal:
     - Acetylene + H2/Lindlar → ethylene; + HBr → EtBr.
     - HC≡CNa + EtBr → 1-butyne; H2/Lindlar → 1-butene; O3/DMS → propanal (+ HCHO).
  2. HC≡CNa + propanal, then H2O → 1-pentyn-3-ol.
  3. H2, Lindlar → 1-penten-3-ol.

### Problem 12.35 (도전 문제) — book p. 565 (PDF p-50) — footnote 4 → J. Agric. Food Chem. 1983, 31, 1287–1292
- Target: tetradecane (C14 linear; one of 130 volatiles from fried chicken). Acetylene is the only carbon source.
- Route [M]:
  1. Make 1-bromohexane by iterative two-carbon homologation:
     - EtBr (acetylene → H2/Lindlar → HBr).
     - HC≡CNa + EtBr → 1-butyne; Lindlar → 1-butene; HBr/ROOR → 1-bromobutane.
     - HC≡CNa + 1-bromobutane → 1-hexyne; Lindlar → 1-hexene; HBr/ROOR → 1-bromohexane.
  2. Dialkylate acetylene (NaNH2 / 1-bromohexane, twice) → 7-tetradecyne.
  3. H2, Pt → tetradecane.
- Many equivalent routes are possible.

### Problem 12.36 (도전 문제) — book p. 565 (PDF p-50) — footnote 5 → J. Nat. Prod. 2012, 75, 1765–1776
- Targets: 2-decanone (`CCCCCCCCC(C)=O`) and decanal (`CCCCCCCCCC=O`), structural isomers among 254 microbial volatiles. Carbon sources: only alkenes with fewer than 6 carbons.
- Common intermediate: 1-decyne.
  - 2-Decanone: H2SO4, H2O, HgSO4 (Markovnikov hydration).
  - Decanal: 1) R2BH (disiamylborane/9-BBN); 2) H2O2, NaOH.
- Making 1-decyne [M]:
  - Acetylide from ethylene: Br2; excess NaNH2.
  - 1-Bromooctane by iterative homologation: 1-butene → HBr/ROOR → BuBr; HC≡CNa → 1-hexyne; Lindlar → 1-hexene; HBr/ROOR → 1-bromohexane; repeat → 1-bromooctane.
  - HC≡CNa + 1-bromooctane → 1-decyne.
- The book's intended route may differ.

### Problem 12.37 (도전 문제) — book p. 565 (PDF p-50) — footnote 6 → Nat. Prod. Rep. 2012, 29, 1288–1303
- Target: (Z)-3-hexenyl acetate, `CC/C=C\CCOC(C)=O` (green-leaf volatile released by wounded Arabidopsis). Carbon sources: only ethylene and acetic acid. Must use the acetylide + epoxide reaction (12.27).
- Route [M–H]:
  1. Ethylene → Br2; excess NaNH2 → acetylene.
  2. Ethylene + HBr → EtBr; HC≡CNa + EtBr → 1-butyne.
  3. NaNH2; then ethylene oxide (from ethylene + RCO3H); then H2O → 3-hexyn-1-ol.
  4. H2, Lindlar → (Z)-3-hexen-1-ol.
  5. Acetic acid, H⁺ (Fischer esterification) → target.

### Problem 12.38 (도전 문제) — book p. 565 (PDF p-50) — footnote 7 → J. Agric. Food Chem. 2002, 50, 3401–3404
- Targets: 3-methylbutanal (`CC(C)CC=O`) and hexanal (`CCCCCC=O`) from tomato aroma (367 compounds). Asked: a single synthesis that gives an equimolar mixture of both, starting from one 1°, one 2° and one 3° alcohol, each with fewer than 6 C. (Hint: 1°/2°/3° alcohols are named like alkyl halides.)
- Key idea [H]: ozonolysis (O3; DMS) of (Z)- or (E)-2-methylundec-4-ene, `CC(C)C/C=C\CCCCC`, gives exactly 1:1 3-methylbutanal + hexanal.
- Alkene from an internal alkyne (CH3)2CHCH2–C≡C–(CH2)4CH3, then H2/Lindlar.
- Assembly [L]:
  - Isobutyl bromide from 2-methyl-2-propanol (3°): H2SO4/heat → isobutylene; HBr/ROOR.
  - 1-Bromopentane from 1-pentanol (1°): conc. H2SO4 → 1-pentene; HBr/ROOR. Or HBr directly.
  - The C2 alkyne unit would be acetylene, alkylated twice.
  - I could not identify how the 2° alcohol fits; the intended disconnection is unclear (flag).

### Problem 12.39 (도전 문제) — book p. 565 (PDF p-50) — footnote 8 → Actinomycetologica 2009, 23, 27–33
- Target: methyl 4-methylpentanoate, `COC(=O)CCC(C)C` (Streptomyces volatile). Starting material: 2-methylbutane, `CCC(C)C`.
- Route [M]:
  1. Br2, hν → 2-bromo-2-methylbutane.
  2. NaOEt → 2-methyl-2-butene.
  3. HBr, ROOR → 2-bromo-3-methylbutane.
  4. t-BuOK (Hofmann) → 3-methyl-1-butene.
  5. HBr, ROOR → 1-bromo-3-methylbutane.
  6. HC≡CNa → 5-methyl-1-hexyne.
  7. 1) O3; 2) H2O → 4-methylpentanoic acid + CO2.
  8. CH3OH, H⁺ (Fischer) → methyl ester.
- Net: +2 C from the acetylide, −1 C lost by ozonolysis.

### Problem 12.40 (도전 문제) — book p. 565 (PDF p-50) — footnote 9 → J. Agric. Food Chem. 2009, 57, 3818–3830
- Target: (E)-2-hexenal, `CCC/C=C/C=O` (Cabernet Sauvignon grape volatile). Starting material: 1,1-dibromopentane, `CCCCC(Br)Br`. Use the 12.28 chemistry (acetylide + carbonyl).
- Route [M]:
  1. Excess NaNH2 → pentynide (1-pentyne anion).
  2. H2C=O; then H2O → 2-hexyn-1-ol.
  3. Na, NH3(l) → (E)-2-hexen-1-ol.
  4. Oxidation (PCC; next chapter) → (E)-2-hexenal.

### Problem 12.41 (도전 문제) — book pp. 565–566 (PDF p-50/51) — footnote 10 → Tetrahedron 1977, 33, 2447–2450 (dermestid beetle sex pheromone)
- Starting material 1: 5-methylhept-1-ene, `C=CCC[C@@H](C)CC`. The stereocenter is drawn with a hashed methyl and computes as S as drawn (RDKit).
- Sequence [H for constitution]:
  - 1 → 2 (BH3·THF): trialkylborane (5-methylheptyl)3B (anti-Markovnikov).
  - 2 → 3 (1) Br2; 2) NaOMe): alkyl bromide 3 = 1-bromo-5-methylheptane, `BrCCCC[C@@H](C)CC` (given).
  - 3 → 4 (HC≡CLi, DMSO): SN2 → 7-methylnon-1-yne, `C#CCCCC[C@@H](C)CC`.
  - 4 → 5 (1) BuLi; 2) I(CH2)7OTHP): THPO(CH2)7C≡C(CH2)4CH(CH3)CH2CH3.
  - 5 → 6 (TsOH, MeOH; THP removal): 14-methylhexadec-8-yn-1-ol, `OCCCCCCCC#CCCCC[C@@H](C)CC` (C17H32O).
  - 6 → 7 (H2, Lindlar): (Z)-14-methylhexadec-8-en-1-ol, `OCCCCCCC/C=C\CCCC[C@@H](C)CC` (C17H34O), a Trogoderma pheromone.
- The stereocenter is untouched throughout, and its CIP label stays the same in every compound.
- Caveat: the literature natural pheromone is often cited as (R,Z), while the book's hashed drawing computes as S. Check the drawing direction [M].

# 제13장 알코올과 Phenol (Alcohols and Phenols)

## Key reactions (summary pages 609–612)
- Alcohol → alkyl chloride with inversion: SOCl2/py (R→S shown); HCl/ZnCl2 also shown in the summary scheme.
- E1/E2: tertiary alcohol + conc. H2SO4, heat → alkene + H2O; ROH → TsCl/py → ROTs → NaOEt → alkene (E2).
- Oxidation: 2° alcohol + Na2Cr2O7/H2SO4/H2O → ketone. 1° alcohol + Na2Cr2O7 (chromic acid) → carboxylic acid. 1° alcohol + PCC/CH2Cl2, DMP/CH2Cl2, or Swern (1) DMSO, (COCl)2; 2) Et3N) → aldehyde. Phenol + Na2Cr2O7/H2SO4 → benzoquinone.
- Concept review (13.1–13.13): alkoxide/phenoxide, pKa 15–18; NaBH4 vs LiAlH4 (LiAlH4 reduces acids/esters); Grignard + ketone/aldehyde/ester (ester adds 2 R); TMS protecting group for Grignard; SN1 (3°) vs SN2 (1°/2°: HX, SOCl2, PBr3, or TsCl then nucleophile); oxidation of 1°/2°/3° alcohols; NADH/NAD+; phenol → quinone; synthesis = carbon skeleton change + functional-group change.
- Skill builders 13.1–13.9: naming, acidity (resonance/induction/solvation), oxidation state, LiAlH4 mechanism, Grignard retrosynthesis, ROH→RCl (TsCl,py then NaCl; or SOCl2/py, inversion), oxidation products, functional-group interconversion map, synthesis design.

## Literature-footnoted problems, Ch.13

### Ch.13 참고문헌 (p.617), verbatim
1. J. Org. Chem. **2012**, 77, 3390–3400.
2. J. Org. Chem. **2000**, 65, 9249–9251.
3. J. Agric. Food Chem. **1986**, 34, 140–144.
4. Tetrahedron Lett. **2007**, 48, 8479–8481.
5. J. Org. Chem. **2009**, 74, 5458–5470.
6. J. Nat. Prod. **2011**, 74, 1262–1267.

8. J. Nat. Prod. **1996**, 59, 49–51.
9. J. Chem. Ecol. **2012**, 38, 814–824.

**Refs 1–3 are not cited in any problem on these pages.** No end-of-chapter problem from 13.28 to 13.73 has footnote marks 1, 2 or 3. The first footnote mark in the problems is "4", in 13.63. Footnotes 1–3 must belong to the Ch.13 body text (book pp. ~560–608), which is not in this PDF. I did not attach them to any compound.

### 13.63 — (S)-Gizzerosine synthesis (footnote 4) — book p.615 (p-07)
- Citation: ref 4, Tetrahedron Lett. 2007, 48, 8479–8481.
- Context: (S)-gizzerosine is an amino acid that causes "black vomit" in chickens; it is a possible drug for osteoporosis and hyperacidity. This step comes from a recent synthesis.
- Starting material: methyl (S,E)-2-(Boc-amino)-6-oxohex-4-enoate, i.e. OHC–CH=CH–CH2–CH(NHBoc)–CO2CH3.
  SMILES `O=C/C=C/C[C@H](NC(=O)OC(C)(C)C)C(=O)OC` (S, confirmed by RDKit; E is assumed from the trans drawing)
- Product: methyl (S)-2-(Boc-amino)-6-hydroxyhexanoate, HO–(CH2)4–CH(NHBoc)–CO2CH3.
  SMILES `OCCCC[C@H](NC(=O)OC(C)(C)C)C(=O)OC`
- Asked: propose the 2-step ("2 단계") synthesis.
- Concept: chemoselective reduction. Reduce the C=C and the aldehyde, but keep the methyl ester and the Boc carbamate.
- Expected answer (high): 1) H2, Pd/C (or Pt, Ni) to hydrogenate the C=C; 2) NaBH4/MeOH to reduce the aldehyde to the 1° alcohol (NaBH4 leaves the ester alone; LiAlH4 must not be used). Doing NaBH4 first and then H2/Pd is also acceptable.

### 13.68 — Briarellin E (footnote 5) — p.616 (p-08)
- Citation: ref 5, J. Org. Chem. 2009, 74, 5458–5470.
- Context: briarellin E (compound 3) is a marine natural product from Caribbean and Mediterranean corals, studied as a potential anticancer agent.
- Reaction: compound 1 (the briarellin core: tricyclic ether-bridged diterpenoid, cyclohexene with methyls, oxacycle, exocyclic methylene, with **two O–TBS ethers** (Si(CH3)2C(CH3)3) and one free OH) **+ TBAF → 2** → (further steps) → 3 (briarellin E, which carries an octanoate ester and an extra ring OH).
- Asked: draw compound 2.
- Concept: fluoride removes silyl protecting groups (Si–F bond strength).
- Expected answer (high): 2 = 1 with **both OTBS groups turned into OH** (a triol: the two former silyl ethers plus the existing HO). The skeleton and all stereocenters stay the same. No SMILES given because the polycyclic stereostructure cannot be read reliably at this resolution.

### 13.69 — Duryne (footnote 6) — p.616 (p-08)
- Citation: ref 6, J. Nat. Prod. 2011, 74, 1262–1267.
- Context: duryne is one of many compounds isolated from a marine sponge.
- Target: HC≡C–CH(OH)–CH=CH–(CH2)9–CH=CH–(CH2)9–CH=CH–CH(OH)–C≡CH (C30). As drawn, the outer alkenes are E (trans) and the central alkene is Z (cis).
  SMILES (stereocenters unspecified) `C#CC(O)/C=C/CCCCCCCCC/C=C\CCCCCCCCC/C=C/C(O)C#C`
- Asked: propose an efficient synthesis from compounds with ≤ 11 carbons.
- Concept: alkyne chemistry (acetylide alkylation; Lindlar → cis; Na/NH3 → trans) plus Grignard addition of an acetylide to an aldehyde. The molecule is symmetric, so it can be built from two identical halves.
- Expected answer (medium, design question): build the central cis alkene from acetylene by double alkylation with two C≥9 halides (e.g. a protected X–(CH2)9– fragment), then H2/Lindlar. At each end: E-CH=CH–CHO (from an alkyne reduced with Na/NH3 or built from C≤11 pieces) + HC≡CMgBr (or HC≡CNa), then H2O, gives the propargylic alcohols. Several routes are acceptable.

### 13.70 — Estragole (footnote 7) — p.617 (p-09)
- Citation: ref 7, J. Nat. Prod. 1987, 50, 990–991.
- Context: estragole was isolated from the leaves of the *Clausena anisata* tree as an insect repellent.
- Starting material: 4-methylphenol (p-cresol) `Cc1ccc(O)cc1`
- Target: estragole, 4-allylanisole `C=CCc1ccc(OC)cc1` (C10H12O)
- Asked: propose a synthesis.
- Concept: phenol acidity (phenoxide + MeI), benzylic radical bromination, Grignard, functional-group interconversion.
- Expected answer (medium, one acceptable route): 1) NaOH (or NaH), CH3I → 4-methylanisole; 2) NBS (or Br2), hν → 4-MeO-C6H4CH2Br; 3) Mg → ArCH2MgBr; 4) ethylene oxide (or H2C=O, then repeat the homologation), then H3O+ → ArCH2CH2CH2OH; 5) TsCl/py, then t-BuOK (bulky base, Hofmann-type E2) → ArCH2CH=CH2. Alternative: ArCH2MgBr + CH2=CH–CH2Br-type coupling. Any route that adds the allyl unit and methylates the phenol O is acceptable.

### 13.71 — Ketone isomers from marijuana / hops volatile oil (footnote 8) — p.617 (p-09)
- Citation: ref 8, J. Nat. Prod. 1996, 59, 49–51.
- Context: two ketone isomers are among 68 compounds in the volatile oil from dried marijuana and hops.
- Targets: (i) **2-methylheptan-4-one** `CC(C)CC(=O)CCC`; (ii) **3-methylheptan-4-one** `CCC(C)C(=O)CCC` (both C8H16O).
- Asked: propose a synthesis of each from alkenes with (no more than) 4 carbons. The Korean text reads "4개의 탄소원자를 가지는 … 알켄을 이용하여".
- Concept: alkene → alkyl halide or alcohol, Grignard + aldehyde, then oxidation of the 2° alcohol.
- Expected answer (high):
  - Shared piece: butanal from 1-butene: 1) BH3·THF; 2) H2O2, NaOH → 1-butanol; then PCC (or DMP/Swern) → butanal.
  - (i) 2-methylpropene + HBr/ROOR → isobutyl bromide → Mg → i-BuMgBr + butanal → H2O → 2-methylheptan-4-ol → Na2Cr2O7/H2SO4 (or PCC) → 2-methylheptan-4-one.
  - (ii) 2-butene + HBr → 2-bromobutane → Mg → sec-BuMgBr + butanal → 3-methylheptan-4-ol → oxidize → 3-methylheptan-4-one.

### 13.72 — 5,9,12,16-Tetramethyleicosane, Brazilian (tomato) bug pheromone (footnote 9) — p.617 (p-09)
- Citation: ref 9, J. Chem. Ecol. 2012, 38, 814–824.
- Context: this is a male sex pheromone of a Brazilian bug that eats tomato leaves and fruit. Building blocks: **A** 1-bromobutane `CCCCBr`; **B** cyclopropyl methyl ketone `CC(=O)C1CC1`; **C** hexane-2,5-dione `CC(=O)CCC(C)=O`.
- Key step: 2-cyclopropylhexan-2-ol `CCCCC(C)(O)C1CC1` + **HBr** → (E/Z)-1-bromo-4-methyloct-3-ene `CCCC/C(C)=C/CCBr` (E+Z, C9H17Br).
- Asked: (a) mechanism of the HBr step; (b) efficient synthesis of the target `CCCCC(C)CCCC(C)CCC(C)CCCC(C)CCCC` (C24H50, numbering checked) using A, B and C.
- Concept: SN1-type ring opening of a cyclopropylcarbinyl cation to a homoallylic bromide (Julia-type), then Grignard, dehydration and hydrogenation.
- Expected answer (high):
  - (a) Protonate the OH and lose H2O to get a tertiary cyclopropylcarbinyl cation. Br⁻ attacks a cyclopropane CH2 and the ring C–C bond breaks (cyclopropylcarbinyl → homoallyl, relief of ring strain), giving C=C at the former carbinol carbon. The trisubstituted alkene forms as an E/Z mixture.
  - (b) A + Mg → BuMgBr. BuMgBr + B → 2-cyclopropylhexan-2-ol. HBr → homoallylic bromide (C9). Mg → Grignard. 2 equiv of that Grignard + C (hexanedione), then H3O+ → C24 tertiary diol. Conc. H2SO4/heat (dehydration) → polyene. H2, Pd/C → 5,9,12,16-tetramethyleicosane. Carbon count: 9 + 6 + 9 = 24, and the methyls land at 5, 9, 12, 16.

### 13.73 — Brevetoxin B, K-ring fragment (Nicolaou) (footnote 10) — p.617 (p-09)
- Citation: ref 10, J. Am. Chem. Soc. 1989, 111, 6682–6690.
- Context: brevetoxin B (2) is a red-tide neurotoxin from *Ptychodiscus brevis* Davis. Nicolaou's synthesis builds the K ring from compound 1. In 1, the syn arrangement of the hydroxyl and the silicon-based protecting group makes the conversion 1 → 5 possible. The step 3 → 4 is a selective tosylation of the primary OH.
- Starting material 1: a tetrahydropyran (K ring). Going round the ring: O1; C2 (H, allyl CH2CH=CH2); C3 (CH3, tertiary OH); C4 (OCH2Ph); C5H2; C6 (CH2–O–SiR3).
  SMILES (stereo omitted) `C=CCC1OC(CO[Si](R)(R)R)CC(OCc2ccccc2)C1(C)O`
- Sequence: 1 + TBAF → 3; TsCl, pyridine → 4; NaOMe → 5 (a bridged bicyclic ether, drawn as 2,6-dioxabicyclo[2.2.2]octane with OBn, Me, allyl).
- Asked: (a) structures of 3 and 4; (b) mechanism 4 → 5 with NaOMe; (c) how the conversion 1 → 5 relates to the syn arrangement of OH and the silyl group in 1.
- Expected answer (high):
  - (a) 3 = the diol (CH2OSiR3 → CH2OH). SMILES `C=CCC1OC(CO)CC(OCc2ccccc2)C1(C)O`. 4 = the primary tosylate (only the 1° OH reacts; the 3° OH is too hindered). SMILES `C=CCC1OC(COS(=O)(=O)c2ccc(C)cc2)CC(OCc2ccccc2)C1(C)O`.
  - (b) NaOMe deprotonates the 3° OH. The alkoxide does an intramolecular SN2 (Williamson) on CH2–OTs and gives the bridged ether 5. The ring flips to a boat so that C3–O⁻ and C6–CH2OTs reach each other across the ring.
  - (c) Ring closure is only possible because the C3–OH and the C6–CH2OSiR3/CH2OTs are cis (syn, same face) on the K ring. In the trans isomer the two groups cannot reach each other.

Note: 13.64–13.67 are spectroscopy problems with no footnotes. No other Ch.13 problem has a literature mark.
